Well… I guess what finally made me come in is this whole historical society fundraiser I got myself wrapped up in. I took it on like I could practically run the entire thing by myself, and at the time it felt very doable, like I had all these ideas and energy for it.

I started making quilt designs, calling volunteers, writing out schedules, telling people when things would be ready… and I was promising deadlines that, looking back, I really couldn’t possibly meet. It just kept growing because I’d think of another thing that needed doing and decide, “Oh, I’ll handle that too.” And then suddenly I’m the one coordinating everything, or trying to, and it’s gotten kind of out of hand.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I think so. I mean, the fundraiser is the clearest example because it’s right in front of me and it’s the thing everyone can point to, but the pattern is… I get an idea and then I start seeing all the pieces of it at once, and instead of saying, “That’s too much,” I think, “No, I can do that, and that, and that too.”

With the historical society thing, I didn’t just agree to help a little. I started acting like I could run the entire fundraiser myself. I was sketching quilt designs, calling volunteers, making schedules, telling people when things would be done, and promising deadlines that, honestly, I don’t know how I thought I could meet. At the time it didn’t feel unreasonable. It felt like I was being productive and helpful, like I had the energy for it and could keep all the parts moving.

But then it just kept expanding. I’d think of one more thing that needed doing, and instead of asking someone else or waiting, I’d take that on too. So yes, I guess I do take on too much like that, especially when I get really caught up in something and feel like I can organize the whole thing if I just push hard enough.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n2\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F60.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what finally made me come in is this whole historical society fundraiser I got myself wrapped up in. I took it on like I could practically run the entire thing by myself, and at the time it felt very doable, like I had all these ideas and energy for it.\n\nI started making quilt designs, calling volunteers, writing out schedules, telling people when things would be ready… and I was promising deadlines that, looking back, I really couldn’t possibly meet. It just kept growing because I’d think of another thing that needed doing and decide, “Oh, I’ll handle that too.” And then suddenly I’m the one coordinating everything, or trying to, and it’s gotten kind of out of hand.\n\nDoctor: Have you often found yourself taking on too much like this in other situations, not just the historical society fundraiser?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I mean, the fundraiser is the clearest example because it’s right in front of me and it’s the thing everyone can point to, but the pattern is… I get an idea and then I start seeing all the pieces of it at once, and instead of saying, “That’s too much,” I think, “No, I can do that, and that, and that too.”\n\nWith the historical society thing, I didn’t just agree to help a little. I started acting like I could run the entire fundraiser myself. I was sketching quilt designs, calling volunteers, making schedules, telling people when things would be done, and promising deadlines that, honestly, I don’t know how I thought I could meet. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… yes, it has. I think I didn’t want to admit how much, because I kept telling myself I was just busy or that I’d catch up if I had one more day, but it’s caused real problems.

My husband has had to step in with things at home that I normally would’ve kept track of—just the routines, the basic household things—because I was either too tangled up in the fundraiser or, later, so overwhelmed by what I’d promised that I wasn’t keeping up with anything very well. And with the historical society, it got embarrassing. I had told people things would be ready, or that I’d call them back, or that I’d have decisions made, and then I just… didn’t. I stopped keeping the commitments I’d made.

And then once I knew people were waiting on me, I couldn’t face them. I started avoiding it, which only made it worse, because then my husband ended up having to talk to people and smooth things over or explain that I wasn’t able to manage it. That felt awful, because I’m a grown woman, and these were things I had volunteered for. I really thought I could handle it, and then suddenly he was the one cleaning up the mess I made. So yes, it’s been stressful, and it’s definitely caused problems.

on the map, like I had finally found the thing only I could make happen.\n- key: S037\n  name: Pressured_Speech\n  description: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.\n  manifestation: Episode Duration\n  description: 9 months\n  manifestation: Since the orders fell behind and the volunteer conflicts blew up, I’ve been stuck in this low, guilty place for about nine months.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 59 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S040\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what finally made me come in is this whole historical society fundraiser I got myself wrapped up in. I took it on like I could practically run the entire thing by myself, and at the time it felt very doable, like I had all these ideas and energy for it.\n\nI started making quilt designs, calling volunteers, writing out schedules, telling people when things would be ready… and I was promising deadlines that, looking back, I really couldn’t possibly meet. It just kept growing because I’d think of another thing that needed doing and decide, “Oh, I’ll handle that too.” And then suddenly I’m the one coordinating everything, or trying to, and it’s gotten kind of out of hand.\n\nDoctor: Have you often found yourself taking on too much like this in other situations, not just the historical society fundraiser?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I mean, the fundraiser is the clearest example because it’s right in front of me and it’s the thing everyone can point to, but the pattern is… I get an idea and then I start seeing all the pieces of it at once, and instead of saying, “That’s too much,” I think, “No, I can do that, and that, and that too.”\n\nWith the historical society thing, I didn’t just agree to help a little. I started acting like I could run the entire fundraiser myself. I was sketching quilt designs, calling volunteers, making schedules, telling people when things would be done, and promising deadlines that, honestly, I don’t know how I thought I could meet. I’d think of one more thing that needed doing, and instead of asking someone else or waiting, I’d take that on too. So yes, I guess I do take on too much like that, especially when I get really caught up in something and feel like I can organize the whole thing if I just push hard enough.\n\nDoctor: Has your tendency to take on too much caused you significant stress or problems in your life?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it has. I think I didn’t want to admit how much, because I kept telling myself I was just busy or that I’d catch up if I had one more day, but it’s caused real problems.\n\nMy husband has had to step in with things at home that I normally would’ve kept track of—just the routines, the basic household things—because I was either too tangled up in the fundraiser or, later, so overwhelmed by what I’d promised that I wasn’t keeping up with anything very well. And with the historical society, it got embarrassing. I had told people things would be ready, or that I’d call them back, or that I’d have decisions made, and then I just… didn’t. I stopped keeping the commitments I’d made.\n\nAnd then once I knew people were waiting on me, I couldn’t face them. I started avoiding it, which only made it worse, because then my husband ended up having to talk to people and smooth things over or explain that I wasn’t able to manage it. That felt awful, because I’m a grown woman, and these were things I had volunteered for. I really thought I could handle it, and then suddenly he was the one cleaning up the mess I made. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d describe it exactly as impulse, but maybe that’s because I’m trying to make it sound more sensible than it was. In the moment, it feels like I’ve thought it through enough, if that makes sense. Like with the fundraiser, I wasn’t sitting down and carefully considering whether I had the time or whether the deadlines were realistic. I just sort of… saw what needed doing and said yes, or told myself I could make it work.

So I don’t know if I’m running off and doing random things without any thought at all. It’s more that I get moving before I’ve really looked at the whole picture. I’ll decide something can be done because it feels possible right then, and only later do I realize I didn’t think through the practical parts—how long it would take, what else I already had going on, whether I’d need help.

I guess my honest answer is: sometimes, yes, but I don’t always recognize it as impulsive when it’s happening. It feels like enthusiasm or being useful, and then afterward it becomes clear I should have paused.

I mean, the fundraiser is the clearest example because it’s right in front of me and it’s the thing everyone can point to, but the pattern is… I get an idea and then I start seeing all the pieces of it at once, and instead of saying, “That’s too much,” I think, “No, I can do that, and that, and that too.”\n\nWith the historical society thing, I didn’t just agree to help a little. I started acting like I could run the entire fundraiser myself. I was sketching quilt designs, calling volunteers, making schedules, telling people when things would be done, and promising deadlines that, honestly, I don’t know how I thought I could meet. At the time it didn’t feel unreasonable. It felt like I was being productive and helpful, like I had the energy for it and could keep all the parts moving.\n\nBut then it just kept expanding. I’d think of one more thing that needed doing, and instead of asking someone else or waiting, I’d take that on too. So yes, I guess I do take on too much like that, especially when I get really caught up in something and feel like I can organize the whole thing if I just push hard enough.\n\nDoctor: Has your tendency to take on too much caused you significant stress or problems in your life?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it has. I think I didn’t want to admit how much, because I kept telling myself I was just busy or that I’d catch up if I had one more day, but it’s caused real problems.\n\nMy husband has had to step in with things at home that I normally would’ve kept track of—just the routines, the basic household things—because I was either too tangled up in the fundraiser or, later, so overwhelmed by what I’d promised that I wasn’t keeping up with anything very well. And with the historical society, it got embarrassing. I had told people things would be ready, or that I’d call them back, or that I’d have decisions made, and then I just… didn’t. I stopped keeping the commitments I’d made.\n\nAnd then once I knew people were waiting on me, I couldn’t face them. I started avoiding it, which only made it worse, because then my husband ended up having to talk to people and smooth things over or explain that I wasn’t able to manage it. That felt awful, because I’m a grown woman, and these were things I had volunteered for. I really thought I could handle it, and then suddenly he was the one cleaning up the mess I made. So yes, it’s been stressful, and it’s definitely caused problems.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it exactly as impulse, but maybe that’s because I’m trying to make it sound more sensible than it was. In the moment, it feels like I’ve thought it through enough, if that makes sense. Like with the fundraiser, I wasn’t sitting down and carefully considering whether I had the time or whether the deadlines were realistic. I just sort of… saw what needed doing and said yes, or told myself I could make it work.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’m running off and doing random things without any thought at all. It’s more that I get moving before I’ve really looked at the whole picture. I’ll decide something can be done because it feels possible right then, and only later do I realize I didn’t think through the practical parts—how long it would take, what else I already had going on, whether I’d need help.\n\nI guess my honest answer is: sometimes, yes, but I don’t always recognize it as impulsive when it’s happening. It feels like enthusiasm or being useful, and then afterward it becomes clear I should have paused.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been this way for as long as you can remember?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"

Not exactly for as long as I can remember, no. I mean, I’ve always been someone who can get very involved in things, but this particular… overdriven way it happened with the historical society project, where I just kept taking on more and more and acting like I could keep the whole thing in my head and manage it all, that was more of a stretch of time.

It went on for about five months, I’d say, before everything really fell apart. And during those months it didn’t feel like one big bad decision. It was more like one thing after another—one more call, one more promise, one more piece of the project I thought I could handle—and then by the end of it I couldn’t keep up with what I’d built around myself. So no, I wouldn’t say I’ve been exactly this way my whole life. This was more like a several-month period that got worse as it went on.

months.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 59 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S040\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Has your tendency to take on too much caused you significant stress or problems in your life?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it has. I think I didn’t want to admit how much, because I kept telling myself I was just busy or that I’d catch up if I had one more day, but it’s caused real problems.\n\nMy husband has had to step in with things at home that I normally would’ve kept track of—just the routines, the basic household things—because I was either too tangled up in the fundraiser or, later, so overwhelmed by what I’d promised that I wasn’t keeping up with anything very well. And with the historical society, it got embarrassing. I had told people things would be ready, or that I’d call them back, or that I’d have decisions made, and then I just… didn’t. I stopped keeping the commitments I’d made.\n\nAnd then once I knew people were waiting on me, I couldn’t face them. I started avoiding it, which only made it worse, because then my husband ended up having to talk to people and smooth things over or explain that I wasn’t able to manage it. That felt awful, because I’m a grown woman, and these were things I had volunteered for. I really thought I could handle it, and then suddenly he was the one cleaning up the mess I made. So yes, it’s been stressful, and it’s definitely caused problems.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it exactly as impulse, but maybe that’s because I’m trying to make it sound more sensible than it was. In the moment, it feels like I’ve thought it through enough, if that makes sense. Like with the fundraiser, I wasn’t sitting down and carefully considering whether I had the time or whether the deadlines were realistic. I just sort of… saw what needed doing and said yes, or told myself I could make it work.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’m running off and doing random things without any thought at all. It’s more that I get moving before I’ve really looked at the whole picture. I’ll decide something can be done because it feels possible right then, and only later do I realize I didn’t think through the practical parts—how long it would take, what else I already had going on, whether I’d need help.\n\nI guess my honest answer is: sometimes, yes, but I don’t always recognize it as impulsive when it’s happening. It feels like enthusiasm or being useful, and then afterward it becomes clear I should have paused.\n\nDoctor: Have you been this way for as long as you can remember?\n\nPatient: Not exactly for as long as I can remember, no. I mean, I’ve always been someone who can get very involved in things, but this particular… overdriven way it happened with the historical society project, where I just kept taking on more and more and acting like I could keep the whole thing in my head and manage it all, that was more of a stretch of time.\n\nIt went on for about five months, I’d say, before everything really fell apart. And during those months it didn’t feel like one big bad decision. It was more like one thing after another—one more call, one more promise, one more piece of the project I thought I could handle—and then by the end of it I couldn’t keep up with what I’d built around myself. So no, I wouldn’t say I’ve been exactly this way my whole life. No, I don’t think I’d say it’s been part of my personality for most of my adult life. I mean, I can be the kind of person who gets involved and wants to help, but this particular pattern—where I was so overdriven and kept saying yes and taking on more than I could possibly manage—that feels more tied to that stretch with the historical society.

That went on for about five months before everything really fell apart. It wasn’t like, “Oh, this is just how I’ve always been for decades.” It was more like during those months I got caught up in it, kept adding more and more, and by the time I realized how much I had taken on, it had already become unmanageable. So no, I wouldn’t call it a lifelong personality thing. It was more that several-month period that got out of hand.

This was more like a several-month period that got worse as it went on.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe this tendency to overcommit and take on too much as something that has been a part of your personality for most of your adult life?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’d say it’s been part of my personality for most of my adult life. I mean, I can be the kind of person who gets involved and wants to help, but this particular pattern—where I was so overdriven and kept saying yes and taking on more than I could possibly manage—that feels more tied to that stretch with the historical society.\n\nThat went on for about five months before everything really fell apart. It wasn’t like, “Oh, this is just how I’ve always been for decades.” It was more like during those months I got caught up in it, kept adding more and more, and by the time I realized how much I had taken on, it had already become unmanageable. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think so. It’s more the losing interest part, or maybe everything feels kind of dulled down. I used to really love going out and checking on my garden—just seeing what was coming up, fussing over little things, looking through seed catalogs and imagining what I’d plant next. And with quilting, I could spend ages looking at fabrics and thinking about what I might make for an entry, what colors would go together, all of that.

Lately, though, I’ll look at the fabric or the seed catalogs and it’s like there’s no spark there. I’m not excited by it the way I normally would be. I just sort of stare at it and feel… not much of anything, which is strange because those are usually the things that feel like mine, if that makes sense. They usually make me feel engaged and settled, and now they just feel flat.

It wasn’t like, “Oh, this is just how I’ve always been for decades.” It was more like during those months I got caught up in it, kept adding more and more, and by the time I realized how much I had taken on, it had already become unmanageable. So no, I wouldn’t call it a lifelong personality thing. I’m not excited by it the way I normally would be. I just sort of stare at it and feel… not much of anything, which is strange because those are usually the things that feel like mine, if that makes sense. It’s been… about nine months now, I think. It started after everything with the orders falling behind and then the volunteer conflicts blowing up. That was when I first really noticed I wasn’t just tired or needing a break — I got stuck in this low, guilty place, like I couldn’t get myself back to how I normally am.

And since then, the garden and quilting have just felt kind of far away from me. I’ll still see the catalogs or the fabric sitting there, and part of me knows I used to love that, but it doesn’t really catch. So, yes, I’d say it’s been going on around nine months, since all of that fell apart.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what finally made me come in is this whole historical society fundraiser I got myself wrapped up in. I took it on like I could practically run the entire thing by myself, and at the time it felt very doable, like I had all these ideas and energy for it.\n\nI started making quilt designs, calling volunteers, writing out schedules, telling people when things would be ready… and I was promising deadlines that, looking back, I really couldn’t possibly meet. It just kept growing because I’d think of another thing that needed doing and decide, “Oh, I’ll handle that too.” And then suddenly I’m the one coordinating everything, or trying to, and it’s gotten kind of out of hand.\n\nDoctor: Have you often found yourself taking on too much like this in other situations, not just the historical society fundraiser?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I mean, the fundraiser is the clearest example because it’s right in front of me and it’s the thing everyone can point to, but the pattern is… I get an idea and then I start seeing all the pieces of it at once, and instead of saying, “That’s too much,” I think, “No, I can do that, and that, and that too.”\n\nWith the historical society thing, I didn’t just agree to help a little. I started acting like I could run the entire fundraiser myself. I was sketching quilt designs, calling volunteers, making schedules, telling people when things would be done, and promising deadlines that, honestly, I don’t know how I thought I could meet. At the time it didn’t feel unreasonable. It felt like I was being productive and helpful, like I had the energy for it and could keep all the parts moving.\n\nBut then it just kept expanding. I’d think of one more thing that needed doing, and instead of asking someone else or waiting, I’d take that on too. So yes, I guess I do take on too much like that, especially when I get really caught up in something and feel like I can organize the whole thing if I just push hard enough.\n\nDoctor: Has your tendency to take on too much caused you significant stress or problems in your life?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it has. I think I didn’t want to admit how much, because I kept telling myself I was just busy or that I’d catch up if I had one more day, but it’s caused real problems.\n\nMy husband has had to step in with things at home that I normally would’ve kept track of—just the routines, the basic household things—because I was either too tangled up in the fundraiser or, later, so overwhelmed by what I’d promised that I wasn’t keeping up with anything very well. And with the historical society, it got embarrassing. I had told people things would be ready, or that I’d call them back, or that I’d have decisions made, and then I just… didn’t. I stopped keeping the commitments I’d made.\n\nAnd then once I knew people were waiting on me, I couldn’t face them. I started avoiding it, which only made it worse, because then my husband ended up having to talk to people and smooth things over or explain that I wasn’t able to manage it. So yes, it’s been stressful, and it’s definitely caused problems.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it exactly as impulse, but maybe that’s because I’m trying to make it sound more sensible than it was. In the moment, it feels like I’ve thought it through enough, if that makes sense. Like with the fundraiser, I wasn’t sitting down and carefully considering whether I had the time or whether the deadlines were realistic. I just sort of… saw what needed doing and said yes, or told myself I could make it work.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’m running off and doing random things without any thought at all. It’s more that I get moving before I’ve really looked at the whole picture. I’ll decide something can be done because it feels possible right then, and only later do I realize I didn’t think through the practical parts—how long it would take, what else I already had going on, whether I’d need help.\n\nI guess my honest answer is: sometimes, yes, but I don’t always recognize it as impulsive when it’s happening. It feels like enthusiasm or being useful, and then afterward it becomes clear I should have paused.\n\nDoctor: Have you been this way for as long as you can remember?\n\nPatient: Not exactly for as long as I can remember, no. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It feels like it’s most of the time now, honestly. I mean, I can still go through the motions a little — I’ll pick up the seed catalog or stand by the garden and look at what needs doing, or I’ll take out fabric and lay it there like I’m going to start thinking about colors — but the feeling just doesn’t really come with it.

Before, those things would pull me in. I’d notice one little plant coming up and then suddenly I’d be out there longer than I meant to be, or I’d look at a piece of fabric and start imagining a whole quilt around it. Lately I just look at it and feel… nothing much at all. Not really excitement, not that warm feeling of wanting to get started. It’s more like I know, in my head, that I used to love these things, but I can’t quite access that part of myself right now.

There might be brief moments where I think, “Oh, that’s pretty,” or “I should do something with that,” but it doesn’t last or turn into real engagement. It sort of fades before I can get anywhere with it.

I mean, the fundraiser is the clearest example because it’s right in front of me and it’s the thing everyone can point to, but the pattern is… I get an idea and then I start seeing all the pieces of it at once, and instead of saying, “That’s too much,” I think, “No, I can do that, and that, and that too.”\n\nWith the historical society thing, I didn’t just agree to help a little. I was sketching quilt designs, calling volunteers, making schedules, telling people when things would be done, and promising deadlines that, honestly, I don’t know how I thought I could meet. I’d think of one more thing that needed doing, and instead of asking someone else or waiting, I’d take that on too. So yes, I guess I do take on too much like that, especially when I get really caught up in something and feel like I can organize the whole thing if I just push hard enough.\n\nDoctor: Has your tendency to take on too much caused you significant stress or problems in your life?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it has. I think I didn’t want to admit how much, because I kept telling myself I was just busy or that I’d catch up if I had one more day, but it’s caused real problems.\n\nMy husband has had to step in with things at home that I normally would’ve kept track of—just the routines, the basic household things—because I was either too tangled up in the fundraiser or, later, so overwhelmed by what I’d promised that I wasn’t keeping up with anything very well. I stopped keeping the commitments I’d made.\n\nAnd then once I knew people were waiting on me, I couldn’t face them. I started avoiding it, which only made it worse, because then my husband ended up having to talk to people and smooth things over or explain that I wasn’t able to manage it. So yes, it’s been stressful, and it’s definitely caused problems.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it exactly as impulse, but maybe that’s because I’m trying to make it sound more sensible than it was. Like with the fundraiser, I wasn’t sitting down and carefully considering whether I had the time or whether the deadlines were realistic. I just sort of… saw what needed doing and said yes, or told myself I could make it work.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’m running off and doing random things without any thought at all. It’s more that I get moving before I’ve really looked at the whole picture. I’ll decide something can be done because it feels possible right then, and only later do I realize I didn’t think through the practical parts—how long it would take, what else I already had going on, whether I’d need help.\n\nI guess my honest answer is: sometimes, yes, but I don’t always recognize it as impulsive when it’s happening. I started acting like I could run the entire fundraiser myself. I was sketching quilt designs, calling volunteers, making schedules, telling people when things would be done, and promising deadlines that, honestly, I don’t know how I thought I could meet. At the time it didn’t feel unreasonable. It felt like I was being productive and helpful, like I had the energy for it and could keep all the parts moving.\n\nBut then it just kept expanding. I’d think of one more thing that needed doing, and instead of asking someone else or waiting, I’d take that on too. So yes, I guess I do take on too much like that, especially when I get really caught up in something and feel like I can organize the whole thing if I just push hard enough.\n\nDoctor: Has your tendency to take on too much caused you significant stress or problems in your life?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it has. I think I didn’t want to admit how much, because I kept telling myself I was just busy or that I’d catch up if I had one more day, but it’s caused real problems.\n\nMy husband has had to step in with things at home that I normally would’ve kept track of—just the routines, the basic household things—because I was either too tangled up in the fundraiser or, later, so overwhelmed by what I’d promised that I wasn’t keeping up with anything very well. And with the historical society, it got embarrassing. I had told people things would be ready, or that I’d call them back, or that I’d have decisions made, and then I just… didn’t. I stopped keeping the commitments I’d made.\n\nAnd then once I knew people were waiting on me, I couldn’t face them. I started avoiding it, which only made it worse, because then my husband ended up having to talk to people and smooth things over or explain that I wasn’t able to manage it. That felt awful, because I’m a grown woman, and these were things I had volunteered for. I really thought I could handle it, and then suddenly he was the one cleaning up the mess I made. So yes, it’s been stressful, and it’s definitely caused problems.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it exactly as impulse, but maybe that’s because I’m trying to make it sound more sensible than it was. In the moment, it feels like I’ve thought it through enough, if that makes sense. Like with the fundraiser, I wasn’t sitting down and carefully considering whether I had the time or whether the deadlines were realistic. I just sort of… saw what needed doing and said yes, or told myself I could make it work.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’m running off and doing random things without any thought at all. It’s more that I get moving before I’ve really looked at the whole picture. I’ll decide something can be done because it feels possible right then, and only later do I realize I didn’t think through the practical parts—how long it would take, what else I already had going on, whether I’d need help.\n\nI guess my honest answer is: sometimes, yes, but I don’t always recognize it as impulsive when it’s happening. It feels like enthusiasm or being useful, and then afterward it becomes clear I should have paused.\n\nDoctor: Have you been this way for as long as you can remember?\n\nPatient: Not exactly for as long as I can remember, no. I mean, I’ve always been someone who can get very involved in things, but this particular… overdriven way it happened with the historical society project, where I just kept taking on more and more and acting like I could keep the whole thing in my head and manage it all, that was more of a stretch of time.\n\nIt went on for about five months, I’d say, before everything really fell apart. And during those months it didn’t feel like one big bad decision. It was more like one thing after another—one more call, one more promise, one more piece of the project I thought I could handle—and then by the end of it I couldn’t keep up with what I’d built around myself. So no, I wouldn’t say I’ve been exactly this way my whole life. This was more like a several-month period that got worse as it went on.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe this tendency to overcommit and take on too much as something that has been a part of your personality for most of your adult life?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’d say it’s been part of my personality for most of my adult life. I mean, I can be the kind of person who gets involved and wants to help, but this particular pattern—where I was so overdriven and kept saying yes and taking on more than I could possibly manage—that feels more tied to that stretch with the historical society.\n\nThat went on for about five months before everything really fell apart. It wasn’t like, “Oh, this is just how I’ve always been for decades.” It was more like during those months I got caught up in it, kept adding more and more, and by the time I realized how much I had taken on, it had already become unmanageable. So no, I wouldn’t call it a lifelong personality thing. It was more that several-month period that got out of hand.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced periods of feeling sad or losing interest in activities you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s more the losing interest part, or maybe everything feels kind of dulled down. I used to really love going out and checking on my garden—just seeing what was coming up, fussing over little things, looking through seed catalogs and imagining what I’d plant next. And with quilting, I could spend ages looking at fabrics and thinking about what I might make for an entry, what colors would go together, all of that.\n\nLately, though, I’ll look at the fabric or the seed catalogs and it’s like there’s no spark there. I’m not excited by it the way I normally would be. I just sort of stare at it and feel… not much of anything, which is strange because those are usually the things that feel like mine, if that makes sense. They usually make me feel engaged and settled, and now they just feel flat.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way about your garden and quilting, like they don't spark interest in you anymore?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about nine months now, I think. It started after everything with the orders falling behind and then the volunteer conflicts blowing up. That was when I first really noticed I wasn’t just tired or needing a break — I got stuck in this low, guilty place, like I couldn’t get myself back to how I normally am.\n\nAnd since then, the garden and quilting have just felt kind of far away from me. I’ll still see the catalogs or the fabric sitting there, and part of me knows I used to love that, but it doesn’t really catch. So, yes, I’d say it’s been going on around nine months, since all of that fell apart.\n\nDoctor: Is this lack of interest in your garden and quilting something that you experience all the time, or are there moments when you still feel engaged with them?\n\nPatient: It feels like it’s most of the time now, honestly. I mean, I can still go through the motions a little — I’ll pick up the seed catalog or stand by the garden and look at what needs doing, or I’ll take out fabric and lay it there like I’m going to start thinking about colors — but the feeling just doesn’t really come with it.\n\nBefore, those things would pull me in. I’d notice one little plant coming up and then suddenly I’d be out there longer than I meant to be, or I’d look at a piece of fabric and start imagining a whole quilt around it. Lately I just look at it and feel… nothing much at all. Not really excitement, not that warm feeling of wanting to get started. It’s more like I know, in my head, that I used to love these things, but I can’t quite access that part of myself right now.\n\nThere might be brief moments where I think, “Oh, that’s pretty,” or “I should do something with that,” but it doesn’t last or turn into real engagement. It sort of fades before I can get anywhere with it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yes… I do. More than I want to admit, probably. It’s not just, “Oh, I’m disappointed I’m not enjoying my hobbies.” It turns into this whole loop in my head where I keep going back over everything I think I ruined.

I’ll be sitting there with the fabric out or looking at the garden, and instead of thinking about colors or plants, my mind goes straight to every late order and every sharp thing I said to the volunteers. I replay it like I’m trying to find the exact moment I messed everything up. And then I tell myself I let the church down, and the historical society, and honestly sometimes it feels like I let the whole town down, which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but that’s how it feels in my head.

So then the quilting and the garden don’t just feel empty — they feel like proof that I’m not the person I thought I was. Like if I can’t even care about the things I used to love, and I couldn’t keep up with responsibilities, then what does that say about me? I know maybe that’s not a fair way to look at it, but I keep landing there anyway.

It feels like enthusiasm or being useful, and then afterward it becomes clear I should have paused.\n\nDoctor: Have you been this way for as long as you can remember?\n\nPatient: Not exactly for as long as I can remember, no. I mean, I’ve always been someone who can get very involved in things, but this particular… overdriven way it happened with the historical society project, where I just kept taking on more and more and acting like I could keep the whole thing in my head and manage it all, that was more of a stretch of time.\n\nIt went on for about five months, I’d say, before everything really fell apart. And during those months it didn’t feel like one big bad decision. It was more like one thing after another—one more call, one more promise, one more piece of the project I thought I could handle—and then by the end of it I couldn’t keep up with what I’d built around myself. So no, I wouldn’t say I’ve been exactly this way my whole life. This was more like a several-month period that got worse as it went on.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe this tendency to overcommit and take on too much as something that has been a part of your personality for most of your adult life?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’d say it’s been part of my personality for most of my adult life. I mean, I can be the kind of person who gets involved and wants to help, but this particular pattern—where I was so overdriven and kept saying yes and taking on more than I could possibly manage—that feels more tied to that stretch with the historical society.\n\nThat went on for about five months before everything really fell apart. It wasn’t like, “Oh, this is just how I’ve always been for decades.” It was more like during those months I got caught up in it, kept adding more and more, and by the time I realized how much I had taken on, it had already become unmanageable. So no, I wouldn’t call it a lifelong personality thing. It was more that several-month period that got out of hand.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced periods of feeling sad or losing interest in activities you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s more the losing interest part, or maybe everything feels kind of dulled down. I used to really love going out and checking on my garden—just seeing what was coming up, fussing over little things, looking through seed catalogs and imagining what I’d plant next. And with quilting, I could spend ages looking at fabrics and thinking about what I might make for an entry, what colors would go together, all of that.\n\nLately, though, I’ll look at the fabric or the seed catalogs and it’s like there’s no spark there. I’m not excited by it the way I normally would be. I just sort of stare at it and feel… not much of anything, which is strange because those are usually the things that feel like mine, if that makes sense. They usually make me feel engaged and settled, and now they just feel flat.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way about your garden and quilting, like they don't spark interest in you anymore?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about nine months now, I think. It started after everything with the orders falling behind and then the volunteer conflicts blowing up. That was when I first really noticed I wasn’t just tired or needing a break — I got stuck in this low, guilty place, like I couldn’t get myself back to how I normally am.\n\nAnd since then, the garden and quilting have just felt kind of far away from me. I’ll still see the catalogs or the fabric sitting there, and part of me knows I used to love that, but it doesn’t really catch. So, yes, I’d say it’s been going on around nine months, since all of that fell apart.\n\nDoctor: Is this lack of interest in your garden and quilting something that you experience all the time, or are there moments when you still feel engaged with them?\n\nPatient: It feels like it’s most of the time now, honestly. I mean, I can still go through the motions a little — I’ll pick up the seed catalog or stand by the garden and look at what needs doing, or I’ll take out fabric and lay it there like I’m going to start thinking about colors — but the feeling just doesn’t really come with it.\n\nBefore, those things would pull me in. I’d notice one little plant coming up and then suddenly I’d be out there longer than I meant to be, or I’d look at a piece of fabric and start imagining a whole quilt around it. Lately I just look at it and feel… nothing much at all. Not really excitement, not that warm feeling of wanting to get started. It’s more like I know, in my head, that I used to love these things, but I can’t quite access that part of myself right now.\n\nThere might be brief moments where I think, “Oh, that’s pretty,” or “I should do something with that,” but it doesn’t last or turn into real engagement. It sort of fades before I can get anywhere with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or worthless about not being able to enjoy your garden and quilting like you used to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. More than I want to admit, probably. It’s not just, “Oh, I’m disappointed I’m not enjoying my hobbies.” It turns into this whole loop in my head where I keep going back over everything I think I ruined.\n\nI’ll be sitting there with the fabric out or looking at the garden, and instead of thinking about colors or plants, my mind goes straight to every late order and every sharp thing I said to the volunteers. I replay it like I’m trying to find the exact moment I messed everything up. And then I tell myself I let the church down, and the historical society, and honestly sometimes it feels like I let the whole town down, which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but that’s how it feels in my head.\n\nSo then the quilting and the garden don’t just feel empty — they feel like proof that I’m not the person I thought I was. Like if I can’t even care about the things I used to love, and I couldn’t keep up with responsibilities, then what does that say about me? I know maybe that’s not a fair way to look at it, but I keep landing there anyway.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I replay it like I’m trying to find the exact moment I messed everything up. Like if I can’t even care about the things I used to love, and I couldn’t keep up with responsibilities, then what does that say about me? I know maybe that’s not a fair way to look at it, but I keep landing there anyway.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever have days where you feel more like your old self and enjoy your garden and quilting again?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 59 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Loss_of_Interest\nmanifestation: \"I used to love checking my garden and working on my own quilt entries, but lately I just look at the fabric and seed catalogs and feel nothing much at all.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really in any steady way, no. I wish I could say there are whole days where it comes back and I’m out there fussing over the garden or losing track of time with a quilt, but lately it’s more like… I remember that I used to be that person, but I don’t feel like her.

I’ll pick up the seed catalogs and flip through them, and I know I used to love doing that — circling things, imagining where I’d put them, planning little changes. Or I’ll look at the fabric for my own quilt entries and think, “This should make me feel something.” But mostly I just look at it and feel nothing much at all. It’s not that I hate it. It’s just flat.

Every once in a while there might be a small flicker, like I notice a pretty color or a plant that’s doing well, but it doesn’t really turn into enjoyment. It fades before I can get pulled in the way I used to. So no, I don’t really have days where I feel like my old self with those things.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what finally made me come in is this whole historical society fundraiser I got myself wrapped up in. I took it on like I could practically run the entire thing by myself, and at the time it felt very doable, like I had all these ideas and energy for it.\n\nI started making quilt designs, calling volunteers, writing out schedules, telling people when things would be ready… and I was promising deadlines that, looking back, I really couldn’t possibly meet. It just kept growing because I’d think of another thing that needed doing and decide, “Oh, I’ll handle that too.” And then suddenly I’m the one coordinating everything, or trying to, and it’s gotten kind of out of hand.\n\nDoctor: Have you often found yourself taking on too much like this in other situations, not just the historical society fundraiser?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I mean, the fundraiser is the clearest example because it’s right in front of me and it’s the thing everyone can point to, but the pattern is… I get an idea and then I start seeing all the pieces of it at once, and instead of saying, “That’s too much,” I think, “No, I can do that, and that, and that too.”\n\nWith the historical society thing, I didn’t just agree to help a little. I started acting like I could run the entire fundraiser myself. I was sketching quilt designs, calling volunteers, making schedules, telling people when things would be done, and promising deadlines that, honestly, I don’t know how I thought I could meet. At the time it didn’t feel unreasonable. It felt like I was being productive and helpful, like I had the energy for it and could keep all the parts moving.\n\nBut then it just kept expanding. I’d think of one more thing that needed doing, and instead of asking someone else or waiting, I’d take that on too. So yes, I guess I do take on too much like that, especially when I get really caught up in something and feel like I can organize the whole thing if I just push hard enough.\n\nDoctor: Has your tendency to take on too much caused you significant stress or problems in your life?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it has. I think I didn’t want to admit how much, because I kept telling myself I was just busy or that I’d catch up if I had one more day, but it’s caused real problems.\n\nMy husband has had to step in with things at home that I normally would’ve kept track of—just the routines, the basic household things—because I was either too tangled up in the fundraiser or, later, so overwhelmed by what I’d promised that I wasn’t keeping up with anything very well. And with the historical society, it got embarrassing. I had told people things would be ready, or that I’d call them back, or that I’d have decisions made, and then I just… didn’t. I stopped keeping the commitments I’d made.\n\nAnd then once I knew people were waiting on me, I couldn’t face them. I started avoiding it, which only made it worse, because then my husband ended up having to talk to people and smooth things over or explain that I wasn’t able to manage it. That felt awful, because I’m a grown woman, and these were things I had volunteered for. I really thought I could handle it, and then suddenly he was the one cleaning up the mess I made. So yes, it’s been stressful, and it’s definitely caused problems.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it exactly as impulse, but maybe that’s because I’m trying to make it sound more sensible than it was. In the moment, it feels like I’ve thought it through enough, if that makes sense. Like with the fundraiser, I wasn’t sitting down and carefully considering whether I had the time or whether the deadlines were realistic. I just sort of… saw what needed doing and said yes, or told myself I could make it work.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’m running off and doing random things without any thought at all. It’s more that I get moving before I’ve really looked at the whole picture. I’ll decide something can be done because it feels possible right then, and only later do I realize I didn’t think through the practical parts—how long it would take, what else I already had going on, whether I’d need help.\n\nI guess my honest answer is: sometimes, yes, but I don’t always recognize it as impulsive when it’s happening. It feels like enthusiasm or being useful, and then afterward it becomes clear I should have paused.\n\nDoctor: Have you been this way for as long as you can remember?\n\nPatient: Not exactly for as long as I can remember, no. I mean, I’ve always been someone who can get very involved in things, but this particular… overdriven way it happened with the historical society project, where I just kept taking on more and more and acting like I could keep the whole thing in my head and manage it all, that was more of a stretch of time.\n\nIt went on for about five months, I’d say, before everything really fell apart. And during those months it didn’t feel like one big bad decision. It was more like one thing after another—one more call, one more promise, one more piece of the project I thought I could handle—and then by the end of it I couldn’t keep up with what I’d built around myself. So no, I wouldn’t say I’ve been exactly this way my whole life. This was more like a several-month period that got worse as it went on.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe this tendency to overcommit and take on too much as something that has been a part of your personality for most of your adult life?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’d say it’s been part of my personality for most of my adult life. I mean, I can be the kind of person who gets involved and wants to help, but this particular pattern—where I was so overdriven and kept saying yes and taking on more than I could possibly manage—that feels more tied to that stretch with the historical society.\n\nThat went on for about five months before everything really fell apart. It wasn’t like, “Oh, this is just how I’ve always been for decades.” It was more like during those months I got caught up in it, kept adding more and more, and by the time I realized how much I had taken on, it had already become unmanageable. So no, I wouldn’t call it a lifelong personality thing. It was more that several-month period that got out of hand.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced periods of feeling sad or losing interest in activities you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s more the losing interest part, or maybe everything feels kind of dulled down. I used to really love going out and checking on my garden—just seeing what was coming up, fussing over little things, looking through seed catalogs and imagining what I’d plant next. And with quilting, I could spend ages looking at fabrics and thinking about what I might make for an entry, what colors would go together, all of that.\n\nLately, though, I’ll look at the fabric or the seed catalogs and it’s like there’s no spark there. I’m not excited by it the way I normally would be. I just sort of stare at it and feel… not much of anything, which is strange because those are usually the things that feel like mine, if that makes sense. They usually make me feel engaged and settled, and now they just feel flat.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way about your garden and quilting, like they don't spark interest in you anymore?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about nine months now, I think. It started after everything with the orders falling behind and then the volunteer conflicts blowing up. That was when I first really noticed I wasn’t just tired or needing a break — I got stuck in this low, guilty place, like I couldn’t get myself back to how I normally am.\n\nAnd since then, the garden and quilting have just felt kind of far away from me. I’ll still see the catalogs or the fabric sitting there, and part of me knows I used to love that, but it doesn’t really catch. So, yes, I’d say it’s been going on around nine months, since all of that fell apart.\n\nDoctor: Is this lack of interest in your garden and quilting something that you experience all the time, or are there moments when you still feel engaged with them?\n\nPatient: It feels like it’s most of the time now, honestly. I mean, I can still go through the motions a little — I’ll pick up the seed catalog or stand by the garden and look at what needs doing, or I’ll take out fabric and lay it there like I’m going to start thinking about colors — but the feeling just doesn’t really come with it.\n\nBefore, those things would pull me in. I’d notice one little plant coming up and then suddenly I’d be out there longer than I meant to be, or I’d look at a piece of fabric and start imagining a whole quilt around it. Lately I just look at it and feel… nothing much at all. Not really excitement, not that warm feeling of wanting to get started. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. I do, and it’s… it’s hard to admit how often it happens, because part of me knows I’m probably being too harsh on myself, but it doesn’t feel that way when I’m in it.

I keep going back over every late order, every thing I didn’t handle right, every sharp thing I said to the volunteers. It’s like my mind won’t leave it alone. I’ll be trying to do something simple, and suddenly I’m replaying it all — what I said, how someone looked at me, what I should have done differently. And then it turns into, “Well, of course everything went wrong, because I failed them.”

I tell myself I let the church down, and the historical society, and honestly sometimes it grows so big in my head that it feels like I let the whole town down. I know that probably sounds exaggerated, but that’s where my thoughts go. It’s not just feeling bad about a mistake. It feels like I am the mistake, if that makes sense. Like I should have been better, steadier, kinder, more capable… and instead I just disappointed everyone.

I think I didn’t want to admit how much, because I kept telling myself I was just busy or that I’d catch up if I had one more day, but it’s caused real problems.\n\nMy husband has had to step in with things at home that I normally would’ve kept track of—just the routines, the basic household things—because I was either too tangled up in the fundraiser or, later, so overwhelmed by what I’d promised that I wasn’t keeping up with anything very well. I just sort of… saw what needed doing and said yes, or told myself I could make it work.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’m running off and doing random things without any thought at all. It’s more that I get moving before I’ve really looked at the whole picture. I’ll decide something can be done because it feels possible right then, and only later do I realize I didn’t think through the practical parts—how long it would take, what else I already had going on, whether I’d need help.\n\nI guess my honest answer is: sometimes, yes, but I don’t always recognize it as impulsive when it’s happening. It feels like enthusiasm or being useful, and then afterward it becomes clear I should have paused.\n\nDoctor: Have you been this way for as long as you can remember?\n\nPatient: Not exactly for as long as I can remember, no. And during those months it didn’t feel like one big bad decision. It was more like one thing after another—one more call, one more promise, one more piece of the project I thought I could handle—and then by the end of it I couldn’t keep up with what I’d built around myself. So no, I wouldn’t say I’ve been exactly this way my whole life. This was more like a several-month period that got worse as it went on.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe this tendency to overcommit and take on too much as something that has been a part of your personality for most of your adult life?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’d say it’s been part of my personality for most of my adult life. I mean, I can be the kind of person who gets involved and wants to help, but this particular pattern—where I was so overdriven and kept saying yes and taking on more than I could possibly manage—that feels more tied to that stretch with the historical society.\n\nThat went on for about five months before everything really fell apart. It wasn’t like, “Oh, this is just how I’ve always been for decades.” It was more like during those months I got caught up in it, kept adding more and more, and by the time I realized how much I had taken on, it had already become unmanageable. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what finally made me come in is this whole historical society fundraiser I got myself wrapped up in. I took it on like I could practically run the entire thing by myself, and at the time it felt very doable, like I had all these ideas and energy for it.\n\nI started making quilt designs, calling volunteers, writing out schedules, telling people when things would be ready… and I was promising deadlines that, looking back, I really couldn’t possibly meet. It just kept growing because I’d think of another thing that needed doing and decide, “Oh, I’ll handle that too.” And then suddenly I’m the one coordinating everything, or trying to, and it’s gotten kind of out of hand.\n\nDoctor: Have you often found yourself taking on too much like this in other situations, not just the historical society fundraiser?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I mean, the fundraiser is the clearest example because it’s right in front of me and it’s the thing everyone can point to, but the pattern is… I get an idea and then I start seeing all the pieces of it at once, and instead of saying, “That’s too much,” I think, “No, I can do that, and that, and that too.”\n\nWith the historical society thing, I didn’t just agree to help a little. I started acting like I could run the entire fundraiser myself. I was sketching quilt designs, calling volunteers, making schedules, telling people when things would be done, and promising deadlines that, honestly, I don’t know how I thought I could meet. At the time it didn’t feel unreasonable. It felt like I was being productive and helpful, like I had the energy for it and could keep all the parts moving.\n\nBut then it just kept expanding. I’d think of one more thing that needed doing, and instead of asking someone else or waiting, I’d take that on too. So yes, I guess I do take on too much like that, especially when I get really caught up in something and feel like I can organize the whole thing if I just push hard enough.\n\nDoctor: Has your tendency to take on too much caused you significant stress or problems in your life?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it has. I think I didn’t want to admit how much, because I kept telling myself I was just busy or that I’d catch up if I had one more day, but it’s caused real problems.\n\nMy husband has had to step in with things at home that I normally would’ve kept track of—just the routines, the basic household things—because I was either too tangled up in the fundraiser or, later, so overwhelmed by what I’d promised that I wasn’t keeping up with anything very well. And with the historical society, it got embarrassing. I had told people things would be ready, or that I’d call them back, or that I’d have decisions made, and then I just… didn’t. I stopped keeping the commitments I’d made.\n\nAnd then once I knew people were waiting on me, I couldn’t face them. I started avoiding it, which only made it worse, because then my husband ended up having to talk to people and smooth things over or explain that I wasn’t able to manage it. That felt awful, because I’m a grown woman, and these were things I had volunteered for. I really thought I could handle it, and then suddenly he was the one cleaning up the mess I made. So yes, it’s been stressful, and it’s definitely caused problems.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it exactly as impulse, but maybe that’s because I’m trying to make it sound more sensible than it was. In the moment, it feels like I’ve thought it through enough, if that makes sense. Like with the fundraiser, I wasn’t sitting down and carefully considering whether I had the time or whether the deadlines were realistic. I just sort of… saw what needed doing and said yes, or told myself I could make it work.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’m running off and doing random things without any thought at all. It’s more that I get moving before I’ve really looked at the whole picture. I’ll decide something can be done because it feels possible right then, and only later do I realize I didn’t think through the practical parts—how long it would take, what else I already had going on, whether I’d need help.\n\nI guess my honest answer is: sometimes, yes, but I don’t always recognize it as impulsive when it’s happening. It feels like enthusiasm or being useful, and then afterward it becomes clear I should have paused.\n\nDoctor: Have you been this way for as long as you can remember?\n\nPatient: Not exactly for as long as I can remember, no. I mean, I’ve always been someone who can get very involved in things, but this particular… overdriven way it happened with the historical society project, where I just kept taking on more and more and acting like I could keep the whole thing in my head and manage it all, that was more of a stretch of time.\n\nIt went on for about five months, I’d say, before everything really fell apart. And during those months it didn’t feel like one big bad decision. It was more like one thing after another—one more call, one more promise, one more piece of the project I thought I could handle—and then by the end of it I couldn’t keep up with what I’d built around myself. So no, I wouldn’t say I’ve been exactly this way my whole life. This was more like a several-month period that got worse as it went on.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe this tendency to overcommit and take on too much as something that has been a part of your personality for most of your adult life?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’d say it’s been part of my personality for most of my adult life. I mean, I can be the kind of person who gets involved and wants to help, but this particular pattern—where I was so overdriven and kept saying yes and taking on more than I could possibly manage—that feels more tied to that stretch with the historical society.\n\nThat went on for about five months before everything really fell apart. It wasn’t like, “Oh, this is just how I’ve always been for decades.” It was more like during those months I got caught up in it, kept adding more and more, and by the time I realized how much I had taken on, it had already become unmanageable. So no, I wouldn’t call it a lifelong personality thing. It was more that several-month period that got out of hand.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced periods of feeling sad or losing interest in activities you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s more the losing interest part, or maybe everything feels kind of dulled down. I used to really love going out and checking on my garden—just seeing what was coming up, fussing over little things, looking through seed catalogs and imagining what I’d plant next. And with quilting, I could spend ages looking at fabrics and thinking about what I might make for an entry, what colors would go together, all of that.\n\nLately, though, I’ll look at the fabric or the seed catalogs and it’s like there’s no spark there. I’m not excited by it the way I normally would be. I just sort of stare at it and feel… not much of anything, which is strange because those are usually the things that feel like mine, if that makes sense. They usually make me feel engaged and settled, and now they just feel flat.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way about your garden and quilting, like they don't spark interest in you anymore?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about nine months now, I think. It started after everything with the orders falling behind and then the volunteer conflicts blowing up. That was when I first really noticed I wasn’t just tired or needing a break — I got stuck in this low, guilty place, like I couldn’t get myself back to how I normally am.\n\nAnd since then, the garden and quilting have just felt kind of far away from me. I’ll still see the catalogs or the fabric sitting there, and part of me knows I used to love that, but it doesn’t really catch. So, yes, I’d say it’s been going on around nine months, since all of that fell apart.\n\nDoctor: Is this lack of interest in your garden and quilting something that you experience all the time, or are there moments when you still feel engaged with them?\n\nPatient: It feels like it’s most of the time now, honestly. I mean, I can still go through the motions a little — I’ll pick up the seed catalog or stand by the garden and look at what needs doing, or I’ll take out fabric and lay it there like I’m going to start thinking about colors — but the feeling just doesn’t really come with it.\n\nBefore, those things would pull me in. I’d notice one little plant coming up and then suddenly I’d be out there longer than I meant to be, or I’d look at a piece of fabric and start imagining a whole quilt around it. Lately I just look at it and feel… nothing much at all. Not really excitement, not that warm feeling of wanting to get started. It’s more like I know, in my head, that I used to love these things, but I can’t quite access that part of myself right now.\n\nThere might be brief moments where I think, “Oh, that’s pretty,” or “I should do something with that,” but it doesn’t last or turn into real engagement. It sort of fades before I can get anywhere with it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or worthless about not being able to enjoy your garden and quilting like you used to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. More than I want to admit, probably. It’s not just, “Oh, I’m disappointed I’m not enjoying my hobbies.” It turns into this whole loop in my head where I keep going back over everything I think I ruined.\n\nI’ll be sitting there with the fabric out or looking at the garden, and instead of thinking about colors or plants, my mind goes straight to every late order and every sharp thing I said to the volunteers. I replay it like I’m trying to find the exact moment I messed everything up. And then I tell myself I let the church down, and the historical society, and honestly sometimes it feels like I let the whole town down, which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but that’s how it feels in my head.\n\nSo then the quilting and the garden don’t just feel empty — they feel like proof that I’m not the person I thought I was. Like if I can’t even care about the things I used to love, and I couldn’t keep up with responsibilities, then what does that say about me? I know maybe that’s not a fair way to look at it, but I keep landing there anyway.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have days where you feel more like your old self and enjoy your garden and quilting again?\n\nPatient: Not really in any steady way, no. I wish I could say there are whole days where it comes back and I’m out there fussing over the garden or losing track of time with a quilt, but lately it’s more like… I remember that I used to be that person, but I don’t feel like her.\n\nI’ll pick up the seed catalogs and flip through them, and I know I used to love doing that — circling things, imagining where I’d put them, planning little changes. Or I’ll look at the fabric for my own quilt entries and think, “This should make me feel something.” But mostly I just look at it and feel nothing much at all. It’s not that I hate it. It’s just flat.\n\nEvery once in a while there might be a small flicker, like I notice a pretty color or a plant that’s doing well, but it doesn’t really turn into enjoyment. It fades before I can get pulled in the way I used to. So no, I don’t really have days where I feel like my old self with those things.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worthless or that you're a failure?\n\nPatient: Yes. I do, and it’s… it’s hard to admit how often it happens, because part of me knows I’m probably being too harsh on myself, but it doesn’t feel that way when I’m in it.\n\nI keep going back over every late order, every thing I didn’t handle right, every sharp thing I said to the volunteers. It’s like my mind won’t leave it alone. I’ll be trying to do something simple, and suddenly I’m replaying it all — what I said, how someone looked at me, what I should have done differently. And then it turns into, “Well, of course everything went wrong, because I failed them.”\n\nI tell myself I let the church down, and the historical society, and honestly sometimes it grows so big in my head that it feels like I let the whole town down. I know that probably sounds exaggerated, but that’s where my thoughts go. It’s not just feeling bad about a mistake. It feels like I am the mistake, if that makes sense. Like I should have been better, steadier, kinder, more capable… and instead I just disappointed everyone.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Most of the time, yes… or at least it feels like it’s right there under everything. I can be doing something ordinary and then my mind goes straight back to all of it — every late order, every time I snapped or said something sharp to one of the volunteers, every moment I should’ve handled better.

I keep telling myself I let the church down, and the historical society, and then somehow in my head it becomes like I let the whole town down. I know that sounds like too much when I say it out loud, but that’s honestly where my mind goes.

So yes… I do feel worthless a lot of the time. Not every single second maybe, but enough that it feels like it’s become the main story I’m telling myself.
